1	# 302 — Per-task slot store with wire-held references
2	
3	## Status
4	open
5	
6	## Current behavior
7	Wire values are passed through stdout pipes and transient strings. Values
8	have no addressable location, no reference count, and no cross-language
9	accessibility. Each value vanishes once the pipe is drained.
10	
11	## Concept
12	
13	### Slots belong to input ports, not tasks
14	
15	Each box has **one slot per input port**, allocated when the graph
16	loads and persisting for the life of the run. A wire is a routing
17	declaration: "the producer's output port pushes a copy of its value
18	into this consumer's named input slot." The producer never owns a
19	slot — its output is a routing event, not a stored value.
20	
21	When a producer fires, the dispatch layer copies the value into
22	every downstream input slot wired to that output. Fan-out is N
23	pushes (one per consumer); fan-in is N producers all pushing into
24	the same consumer's slot. Both behaviors fall out of the same
25	push-into-input-slot mechanic.
26	
27	Slots live in process heap memory, accessible to every worker
28	thread. Cross-process visibility is not required: language specs
29	(issue 303) only ever receive byte pointers — never raw slot
30	addresses — so out-of-process specs (Bash) work via sockets, not
31	shared memory.
32	
33	### Tasks are ephemeral; box state is durable
34	
35	A **task** is one invocation: pop one value from each of the box's
36	input slots, run the box's logic, push the output to every
37	downstream input slot. Tasks have no persistent identity — they're
38	just stack frames on workers. Tasks are spawned dynamically by the
39	dispatch layer when a box's input set is ready (every input slot
40	holds at least one value).
41	
42	The **box** is the durable thing: it owns its input port slots, any
43	counter state (for iterators), its language spec handle, and so
44	on. The box exists from graph load until run end. Tasks come and
45	go.
46	
47	This is uniform across iterator and non-iterator boxes. Both run N
48	times where N depends on how many invocations their inputs supply
49	(possibly 1, possibly thousands). The difference is just what the
50	task does:
51	- **Plain call task**: invoke spec on the popped values, push the
52	  return to outputs.
53	- **Iterator task**: read box's counter, route the popped value to
54	  output port `iterator_outputs[counter]`, increment counter mod N.
55	  No spec invocation.
56	- **Comparator task**: invoke spec on the popped values, compare
57	  the return against `comparand`, route to the matching `lt` /
58	  `eq` / `gt` branch.
59	
60	### All slots are ring buffers; read mode varies
61	
62	There is one slot type. It is a ring buffer of `n_cells` cells,
63	each of `cell_capacity` bytes. The number of cells (`n_cells`) and
64	the read mode are both compile-time properties of the input port:
65	
66	- **1-cell + peek mode**: the value the producer wrote stays in the
67	  cell. Tasks read via `slot_peek` — read without draining — and
68	  every subsequent task on this consumer reads the same value
69	  unless the producer overwrites it. Used for **single-push wires**
70	  (a literal, or a wire from a producer box whose invocation count
71	  is exactly 1). Multiple consumer tasks read the value as many
72	  times as needed; the cell is never empty.
73	- **N-cell + pop mode**: a queue. Producers push; consumers
74	  `slot_pop` — drain one cell per task. Used for **multi-push wires**:
75	  any wire whose producer has more than one task invocation
76	  during the run. The producer's pushes accumulate, the consumer
77	  drains in pop order.
78	
79	Compile-time analysis classifies each wire. A producer is
80	"runs-once" if no transitive ancestor is an iterator; otherwise
81	it's "runs-N-times." Wires from runs-once producers get 1-cell
82	peek slots; wires from runs-N-times producers get N-cell pop slots.
83	
84	`n_cells` for pop slots is sized for worst-case backlog (or a
85	default like 64 with growth on demand). Lists grow if the producer
86	outruns the consumer past the pre-allocation.
87	
88	Fan-in: multiple producers pushing into the same N-cell pop slot
89	land in arrival order. The consumer pops FIFO unless the cell
90	carries an ordering tag (see "Cell tagging" below).
91	
92	### Atomic-counter slot mode
93	
94	A slot allocated with `SLOT_ATOMIC_COUNTER` carries a single
95	4-byte unsigned integer cell, read via a special op:
96	
97	```c
98	uint32_t slot_read_inc(slot_store_t *s, slot_id_t id, uint32_t mod);
99	// Returns the current value mod `mod`, then atomically increments
100	// the underlying counter. Concurrent readers each get a distinct
101	// value. Counter wraps at 2^32; mod handles N-branch routing.
102	```
103	
104	Used for iterator counters (issue 304): the iterator box has an
105	auto-allocated `SLOT_ATOMIC_COUNTER` slot at load time; the
106	dispatch action calls `slot_read_inc(counter_slot, n_branches)`
107	to pick its output branch. Multiple iterator tasks can read
108	concurrently from the same counter slot in parallel — each gets a
109	unique routing index — without any per-box mutable state and
110	without the serial dependency of a self-push counter.
111	
112	The slot's "cell" is just an `atomic_uint32_t`. No push is ever
113	performed; reads do the work. From the dispatch action's
114	perspective it's just another slot; from the slot store's
115	perspective the read op is special-cased.
116	
117	### Cell tagging (for parallel-iterator ordering)
118	
119	A cell can optionally carry a 4-byte ordering tag alongside its
120	size and data:
121	
122	```
123	Cell layout (N-cell pop slot)
124	Offset  Size  Field
125	------  ----  -----
126	0       4     filled_size
127	4       4     tag      (0 if untagged; iterator counter snapshot otherwise)
128	8       …     data
129	```
130	
131	Slots created for wires from parallel iterators (issue 304) are
132	tagged. Pop on a tagged slot returns the cell with the lowest tag,
133	not the head cell — preserving iterator-counter order across pushes
134	that arrived out of order from parallel iterator workers.
135	
136	Untagged slots default tag to 0 and pop FIFO at no extra cost. The
137	tag-aware pop is a per-slot mode flag set at allocation time.
138	
139	### Asynchrony
140	The store is fully asynchronous. Slots are allocated, filled, read, and
141	freed at any time, on any thread, with no global lock. Synchronization
142	is per-slot. There is no central allocator-wide mutex.
143	
144	## Slot layout
145	
146	Each slot is a header followed by `n_cells` cells, each `cell_capacity`
147	bytes. The header tracks ring-buffer state and lifetime:
148	
149	```
150	Slot header
151	Offset  Size  Field
152	------  ----  -----
153	0       4     cell_capacity   (bytes per cell, set at allocation)
154	4       4     n_cells         (ring size, set at allocation)
155	8       4     head            (read index, mod n_cells)
156	12      4     tail            (write index, mod n_cells; cells_filled = tail - head)
157	16      1     touched         (per-slot lock: 0=free, 1=another thread mutating)
158	17      3     _pad
159	20      …     cells           (n_cells * (4-byte size + cell_capacity bytes))
160	```
161	
162	Each cell stores a 4-byte filled-size followed by `cell_capacity`
163	bytes of value data. Cells have wildly different capacities across
164	slots — capacity is per-slot, not global. The C convention of passing
165	sizes alongside arrays applies: the task that allocates a slot knows
166	how big it needs the cells.
167	
168	`touched` is a single-byte flag toggled around any header mutation
169	(push, pop, refcount changes). A reader that finds `touched == 1`
170	spins until it clears. This is the simplest possible per-slot lock —
171	if contention proves to matter, replace with a proper mutex without
172	changing the API.
173	
174	Writes to a cell complete with a memory barrier before `tail` is
175	advanced, so a reader that observes `head < tail` is guaranteed to
176	see the complete value at the head cell.
177	
178	## Lifetime
179	
180	A slot — the named input port on a box — is owned by its box and
181	lives from graph load until run end. That hasn't changed. What
182	has changed is the values that pass through slots: they are
183	reference-counted memory chunks served by a single unified
184	allocator (see "Allocation strategy" below), not malloc'd memory
185	that lives the whole run.
186	
187	Every value handed to a consumer carries a reference. When a
188	consumer is done with a value, it drops its reference. When the
189	last reference drops, the chunk holding the value goes back to
190	the allocator's free-lists, available for the next request. Two
191	neighbor-merge steps and an on-demand sweep keep fragmentation
192	under control (described in the allocator section).
193	
194	The earlier note in this document — "no per-slot reference
195	counting" — referred to the box's own slot record, not to the
196	values flowing through it. The slot record itself is still
197	allocated once at graph load and not freed until run end. The
198	values inside the slot are the things that get refcounted, freed,
199	and recycled. The distinction matters: there's one slot per
200	input port, and many values flow through that slot over the life
201	of a run.
202	
203	Run termination is still governed by the pool's active-task
204	counter (issue 301): when no task is running and no further
205	spawns are pending, the run is over. The allocator and refcount
206	machinery don't influence termination — they just ensure memory
207	gets returned to the free-lists as values become unreferenced
208	during the run.
209	
210	### No wait lists
211	
212	Tasks never park on slots. Tasks are spawned by the dispatch
213	layer's spawn-on-input-arrival rule and run to completion; they
214	never block. A slot that has no value yet simply means the
215	spawn rule hasn't fired for that consumer yet. No wait list,
216	no per-slot wake-up traversal — just a check after each push.
217	
218	## Backing store
219	
220	Slots are allocated from process heap memory. The slot store owns a
221	per-run allocator (described under "Allocation strategy" below) that
222	hands out memory chunks to slots. Plain `malloc`-class memory is
223	sufficient — every worker thread in the process can read and write
224	it, and no spec ever needs to access it from a child process.
225	
226	Synchronization within a slot uses the per-slot `touched` flag for
227	header mutations. There is no allocator-wide mutex on the
228	read/write path; only the per-size-class free lists in the
229	allocator have their own locks.
230	
231	## Slot creation
232	
233	Slot creation happens at **graph load**, not per task. The graph
234	loader walks every box, allocates one ring buffer per input port,
235	and parks the slot on the box's per-port slot table. The dispatch
236	layer never allocates new slots at runtime in the steady state —
237	all slots that will ever exist are created up front.
238	
239	```c
240	slot_id_t slot_alloc(slot_store_t *store, int cell_capacity, int n_cells);
241	```
242	
243	`cell_capacity` and `n_cells` come from compile-time analysis of
244	the graph. Boxes downstream of iterators get larger `n_cells` to
245	absorb the iterator's worst-case backlog.
246	
247	If a slot's pre-allocated `n_cells` proves too small at runtime
248	(producer outpaces consumer beyond the pre-allocation), the slot's
249	ring grows — see "Allocation strategy" below.
250	
251	## C API surface
252	
253	```c
254	slot_id_t slot_alloc       (slot_store_t *s, int cell_capacity, int n_cells, int flags);
255	void      slot_push        (slot_store_t *s, slot_id_t id, const void *data, int size, uint32_t tag);
256	int       slot_peek        (slot_store_t *s, slot_id_t id, void *buf, int buf_size); // 1-cell: read without draining
257	int       slot_pop         (slot_store_t *s, slot_id_t id, void *buf, int buf_size); // N-cell: drain head (or lowest-tag)
258	uint32_t  slot_read_inc    (slot_store_t *s, slot_id_t id, uint32_t mod);            // SLOT_ATOMIC_COUNTER only
259	int       slot_has_value   (slot_store_t *s, slot_id_t id);                          // for spawn-rule check
260	```
261	
262	Flags include:
263	- `SLOT_TAGGED`: cells carry ordering tags; `slot_pop` returns the
264	  lowest-tag cell.
265	- `SLOT_ATOMIC_COUNTER`: slot holds a single atomic uint32; reads
266	  via `slot_read_inc` only. `slot_push` / `slot_peek` / `slot_pop`
267	  are not valid on atomic-counter slots.
268	
269	Push always takes a `tag` argument; untagged slots ignore it (or
270	treat 0 as "no order").
271	
272	1-cell peek slot use: producer `push` once at startup (literal) or
273	when its single invocation completes (single-push wire). All
274	consumer tasks `peek` to read the value as many times as their box
275	runs. The cell is never empty after the initial push.
276	
277	N-cell pop slot use: producers `push` repeatedly. Consumer tasks
278	`pop` one cell each. For tagged slots, `pop` returns the
279	lowest-tag cell; for untagged, FIFO. Pop on an empty slot is a
280	bug — the spawn rule guarantees the slot is non-empty before a
281	task spawns.
282	
283	There is no `slot_ref` / `slot_unref` — slot lifetime is the run.
284	
285	## Submission timing
286	
287	Tasks are spawned dynamically in response to slot pushes. Each box
288	has small per-box state used by the dispatch layer:
289	
290	```
291	box_runtime_state {
292	    slot_id_t  *input_slots;     // one per input port (set at load)
293	    int        *port_modes;      // PEEK or POP, per input port
294	    int         n_inputs;
295	    slot_id_t   counter_slot;    // iterator only: SLOT_ATOMIC_COUNTER slot;
296	                                 // 0 / unused otherwise
297	};
298	```
299	
300	The spawning rule:
301	- When a producer pushes to one of a box's input slots, the
302	  dispatch layer checks **does every input slot have a value
303	  available?** "Available" means:
304	  - For peek-mode (1-cell) slots: cell has been written at least
305	    once. Once a peek slot is filled, it stays available — every
306	    subsequent spawn-check on this box passes the peek-port check.
307	  - For pop-mode (N-cell) slots: queue is non-empty.
308	- If yes, spawn a `dispatch_task_t` and submit to the pool. The
309	  task pops (or peeks) one value from each input slot when it
310	  runs.
311	- Each push that completes the input set triggers exactly one
312	  spawn. Multiple pushes accumulate in pop slots; each pop
313	  consumes one, and if more remain, the next push (or check)
314	  re-fires the spawn rule.
315	
316	This makes the pool's queue the rate buffer: pushes accumulate
317	in pop slots (possibly multiple per slot), and one task per
318	accumulated value gets spawned.
319	
320	Tasks never block on slots. They're spawned after inputs are
321	ready, run to completion, and disappear. Blocking-and-waking
322	machinery is unnecessary — the spawn-on-arrival rule is the
323	synchronization primitive.
324	
325	The pool's queue is the only queue. There is no separate "blocked
326	tasks" structure.
327	
328	## Allocation strategy: one unified allocator for every value
329	
330	There is one allocator. It serves both values whose size is known
331	from the graph at compile time and values whose size only becomes
332	known when they're produced (LLM responses, dynamic arrays, anything
333	where the upper bound isn't a useful number). The earlier draft of
334	this document split these into two stores — a size-class slot
335	allocator for the known case, a separate "large-value heap" for the
336	unknown case. That split was a mistake. They are the same problem
337	and they reuse the same machinery; treating them as two stores led
338	to the unknown-size path being a monotonically-growing pool that
339	never reclaims, which is a memory leak by design.
340	
341	### How the allocator learns sizes ahead of time
342	
343	Before the run starts, the loader walks every box in the graph and
344	reads the size that box declares for its output. A box that always
345	produces a 32-byte number declares 32. A box that always produces a
346	4-megabyte image declares 4 megabytes. The loader collects every
347	distinct declared size, deduplicates them, and creates one free-list
348	per distinct size. Each list is pre-populated with enough chunks to
349	cover the static demand for that size.
350	
351	Boxes whose output size depends on runtime — the LLM case — declare
352	"size unknown." The loader doesn't create a pre-warmed list for those
353	boxes; it knows their values will be allocated on demand.
354	
355	Both kinds share the same underlying memory region. The difference is
356	only whether the loader had a list pre-warmed for the value's size.
357	
358	### Two doors, one allocator
359	
360	**Door 1 — declared-size allocation.** Given a request for size N,
361	where N matches a declared size class: pop the head of that class's
362	free-list. One step. This is the common path for the vast majority
363	of values; nearly every box in a typical graph has a declared output
364	size.
365	
366	**Door 2 — runtime-size allocation.** Given a request for an unknown
367	size at runtime: find the smallest size class whose chunks are large
368	enough. If that class is exhausted, try the next-larger. If the
369	chunk handed out is oversized for the request, slice the unused tail
370	into a residual chunk and push that residual onto the appropriate
371	smaller free-list. The residual is only kept if it's at least as large
372	as the smallest declared size class — anything smaller is left as
373	internal waste rather than fragmenting the lists with stubs.
374	
375	Both doors fall through to the same fragmentation-recovery and growth
376	machinery below when their free-lists can't satisfy a request.
377	
378	### Reference counting drives deallocation
379	
380	Every value the allocator hands out is reference-counted. The dispatch
381	layer increments the count when handing a value to a consumer and
382	decrements it when the consumer is done. When the count reaches zero,
383	the chunk is returned to the allocator.
384	
385	The return-to-allocator step does two things atomically: pushes the
386	chunk onto the appropriate free-list AND checks the chunk's two
387	physical neighbors. If either neighbor is also free, the deallocator
388	merges them into a larger chunk and pushes the merged chunk onto the
389	free-list for the merged size. This eager neighbor-merge is O(1) and
390	keeps small-scale fragmentation from accumulating on the hot path.
391	
392	Reference counting requires touching every place that hands out a
393	pointer to an allocator-managed value. That cost is paid once. After
394	the wiring exists, both doors above benefit from the same recycling.
395	
396	### When does the bigger sweep run
397	
398	The eager neighbor-merge above catches the common case. It misses
399	patterns where free chunks are scattered with allocated chunks between
400	them — the cheap merge can't see past its immediate neighbors.
401	
402	A deeper sweep that walks the whole address-ordered chunk list
403	looking for runs of contiguous free chunks runs on two deterministic
404	triggers, both decoupled from wall-clock time:
405	
406	1. **At the moment an allocation would otherwise fail.** When a
407	   request can't be satisfied from any free-list — including after
408	   trying larger classes and slicing — the system runs the deep sweep
409	   before falling back to asking the operating system for more memory.
410	   If the sweep produces a chunk large enough to satisfy the request,
411	   the allocation succeeds and no growth happens. This is the
412	   mandatory trigger: the sweep is the immediate alternative to
413	   monotonic growth, and it fires exactly when its work has value.
414	
415	2. **At quiescence — moments when no task is running.** The dispatch
416	   layer already recognizes these moments because it uses them to
417	   decide a run is complete. Between batches is a natural opportunity
418	   because no tasks are reading or writing chunks at that instant.
419	   This is the opportunistic trigger.
420	
421	The mandatory trigger is enough on its own. The opportunistic trigger
422	is a tunable knob that can be enabled later if profiling shows
423	fragmentation surviving the cheap merge faster than the mandatory
424	trigger catches up.
425	
426	A third option was considered — trigger when the count of small free
427	fragments crosses a threshold — but rejected for now. It works, but
428	it needs the threshold tuned, and the two triggers above cover the
429	same ground without that tuning step.
430	
431	### Physical-order chunk list (for the merge steps)
432	
433	Both the eager neighbor-merge and the deeper sweep need to know
434	which chunks are physically adjacent in memory. The size-class
435	free-lists don't carry that information — they're indexed by size,
436	not address.
437	
438	The supporting structure is a doubly-linked list of all chunks (free
439	and allocated) in address order. Splits and merges are O(1) given a
440	chunk pointer. Neighbor lookup is O(1). The cost is pointer-chasing
441	during the deeper sweep, but the sweep only runs at the two triggers
442	above, not on the hot path.
443	
444	If profiling later shows traversal cost hurting at sweep time, the
445	list can be swapped for a sorted-by-address array of `(start, end)`
446	pairs with an auxiliary index — the allocator's external API doesn't
447	change.
448	
449	### Why one allocator instead of two
450	
451	The split design existed because the original two-tier scheme had a
452	"large-value heap" carved out for variable-size payloads, on the
453	theory that mixing variable and fixed sizes in the same pool would
454	complicate the fixed-size path. That reasoning doesn't survive
455	contact with reference counting and free-list recycling: with
456	refcount-driven free, the fixed-size path stays fast because most
457	requests match a declared class and pop in one step regardless of
458	what else is in the region.
459	
460	Splitting them also meant the variable-size pool had to grow
461	forever, because writing the same allocator twice — once with
462	reclamation, once without — was more work than was budgeted in early
463	phase 3. That's the position the current code is in. Unified gets us
464	the reclamation everywhere, with no extra design work.
465	
466	### Growth (last resort)
467	
468	When both doors fail and the deep sweep can't produce a fitting
469	chunk, the allocator grows by asking the operating system for a new
470	region, dividing it into chunks per the declared size classes (or
471	into a single large chunk if the request was for an unknown size
472	beyond all declared classes), and threading the new chunks into the
473	free-lists and the address-ordered list.
474	
475	Growth is the rare case. With pre-warmed free-lists sized to static
476	demand and refcount-driven recycling, a well-behaved graph never
477	reaches growth — every allocation either pops a pre-warmed chunk or
478	recycles one whose last reference just dropped.
479	
480	## No type tags
481	
482	Slots store opaque bytes. The producer wrote N bytes; the consumer
483	reads N bytes. What those bytes mean is the language spec's business
484	on each side, and the wire's metadata in the graph (issue 219's
485	compiler can check producer-output type vs consumer-input type at
486	build time). The slot store does not interpret values — it does not
487	need to know if they are strings, numbers, JSON, images, or anything
488	else. Removed the type-tag field from the header for that reason.
489	
490	## Suggested implementation sequence
491	1. Write `src/slot-store.h` and `src/slot-store.c` — the C API above.
492	2. Unit test single-value slots: thread A writes, thread B reads,
493	   refcount decrements, slot freed when last ref dropped.
494	3. Unit test ring-buffer slots: multiple producers push, single consumer
495	   drains in order, no values lost or duplicated.
496	4. Integrate into `src/008-pool-runner.c`: shared memory created at
497	   startup, store handle passed to the dispatch layer.
498	
499	## Relevant files
500	- `src/008-pool-runner.c` — pool entry point, owns the shared memory region
501	- `libs/task-pool/900-task-pool.h` — pool API (refcounting is complementary)
502	- `issues/301-pool-lifecycle-and-worker-init.md` — pool lifecycle
503	- `issues/304-task-dispatch-layer.md` — task dispatch reads/writes slots
504	- `issues/213-queued-inputs-and-task-model.md` — queued input model that
505	  ring-buffer slots implement
506	- `docs/004-ipc-and-threading.md` — IPC options; shm_open + mmap is Option 2
507	
508	## Implementation log
509	
510	### Core milestone — 2026-05-12
511	
512	What shipped:
513	- `src/009-slot-store.h` — the public API exactly as the design
514	  describes: `slot_alloc` / `slot_push` / `slot_peek` / `slot_pop` /
515	  `slot_read_inc` / `slot_has_value` / `slot_fill_count`. Plus the
516	  store lifecycle pair and a small inspection helper for tests.
517	- `src/009-slot-store.c` — implementation. Plain-malloc per slot
518	  (size-class free lists deferred); ring slots and atomic-counter
519	  slots both supported; the SLOT_FLAG_TAGGED variant of pop scans
520	  for the lowest-tag filled cell. Per-slot spinlock via
521	  `atomic_flag`. Atomic counter via `atomic_fetch_add_explicit`,
522	  no lock.
523	- `tests/009-slot-store-test.c` — 12 unit tests covering peek
524	  semantics, FIFO pop, tagged pop ordering (in-order and
525	  interleaved), atomic counter (sequential + 8-thread concurrent),
526	  bad-argument paths, ring-full / empty edge cases, and a
527	  multi-thread producer/consumer race over 5000 values.
528	- Makefile additions: `test` target builds test binaries via a
529	  pattern rule and runs them. Per-test prerequisites listed
530	  explicitly so each binary links the minimal set of source `.o`s.
531	- File index counter advanced to `009`.
532	
533	Verified clean build and 12/12 tests pass in `make STRICT=1`
534	(`-Werror -Wextra -Wpedantic`).
535	
536	What's deferred to a follow-on within 302:
537	- **Size-class free lists with coalescing.** The current
538	  implementation `malloc`s each slot independently. That's correct
539	  but unoptimized. Slots are allocated once at graph load and never
540	  freed until run end, so under the current usage pattern free-list
541	  reuse buys nothing. Lands once profiling justifies it (or once
542	  long-running maps surface allocation pressure).
543	- **Large-value heap for variable-size payloads.** Slots currently
544	  store fixed-size cells; very large outputs (multi-MB LLM
545	  responses, dynamically-sized arrays) will need the two-tier
546	  scheme described under "Large-value heap" above. Blocks no
547	  in-flight phase 3 work until a graph wants variable-size outputs.
548	
549	Neither deferred item blocks 301 / 304 / 305 / 306 / 307 / 308.
550	
551	### Large-value heap milestone — 2026-05-19
552	
553	The variable-size payload follow-on landed in two layers:
554	
555	1. **`src/015-large-value-heap.{c,h}`** — chunked arena allocator.
556	   Default 64 KB chunks; oversized allocations (larger than the
557	   default chunk) get their own exact-fit chunk so one large value
558	   doesn't bloat the regular bump chunks. Pointer stability is the
559	   load-bearing property: chunks never move once allocated, so a
560	   consumer's pointer survives any number of subsequent producer
561	   allocations. Eight unit tests in
562	   `tests/015-large-value-heap-test.c` cover the create/destroy
563	   round-trip, single + multi alloc within a chunk, oversized
564	   allocations getting their own chunks, pointer stability across
565	   forced growth, zero-size allocations returning non-NULL, the
566	   stats accessors, and an eight-thread / eight-thousand-alloc
567	   concurrency stress test that asserts every returned pointer is
568	   unique and writable.
569	
570	2. **Slot-store integration.** Added `SLOT_FLAG_LARGE_VALUE` to
571	   `slot_alloc`. Cell layout for LARGE_VALUE slots is
572	   `filled_size (4 bytes) + pad/tag (4 bytes) + stable pointer
573	   (8 bytes)` — the 8-byte payload offset keeps the pointer
574	   naturally aligned regardless of whether SLOT_FLAG_TAGGED is
575	   also set. The slot store lazy-creates one shared lvh heap on
576	   the first LARGE_VALUE allocation; the heap is destroyed
577	   alongside the store. Push allocates from the heap *outside*
578	   the slot spinlock (the heap has its own mutex; nesting is
579	   fine, but reducing time-under-spinlock keeps the lock fast for
580	   other producers). Peek and pop follow the cell's pointer and
581	   memcpy the requested bytes back into the caller's buffer.
582	
583	Four new slot-store tests cover the new path:
584	`large_value_push_pop` (100 KB payload, double-peek, then pop);
585	`large_value_buf_too_small` (oversized payload + small dest →
586	-1, cell preserved); `large_value_multi_push_fifo` (three
587	different-size values pushed and popped in order); and
588	`large_value_tagged_ordering` (LARGE_VALUE + TAGGED → pops
589	return lowest-tag-first with bytes intact).
590	
591	What this unblocks: any box that wants to emit a variable-size
592	output can now declare its downstream consumer slots with
593	SLOT_FLAG_LARGE_VALUE and produce payloads whose size isn't
594	known at graph-load time. The wiring from declared box output
595	type to the LARGE_VALUE flag in `graph_attach_runtime` lands as
596	a follow-on once a fixture or real graph requires it; the
597	foundation is in place.
598	
599	### Producer-driven LARGE_VALUE wiring — 2026-05-19
600	
601	The "wiring from declared box output type" deferred above is
602	now in place. During `graph_attach_runtime`, each consumer
603	input slot's flags get OR'd with `SLOT_FLAG_LARGE_VALUE` if any
604	producer feeding that port declares `output_capacity == 0`. The
605	rule covers both data boxes (which default to 0 because the
606	field isn't in their JSON schema) and call boxes that opt into
607	variable-size output by setting `"output_capacity": 0`. A
608	mixed-language fan-in where any producer is variable-size
609	upgrades the whole slot to LARGE_VALUE — the conservative call,
610	since the slot can only carry one format and the LVH path
611	handles small payloads correctly too.
612	
613	A new test in `tests/010-graph-loader-test.c`
614	(`variable_size_producer_lvh_wiring`) attaches the hello fixture
615	with a deliberately tight default_cell_bytes of 512, then
616	asserts a 2 KB push succeeds on greet's `name` port (fed by
617	data box `who`) and fails on greet's `salutation` port (literal
618	only, no producer). The discriminator is the slot's capacity:
619	without LARGE_VALUE the 2 KB push exceeds 512 and is rejected;
620	with it the heap absorbs the bytes and the push lands.
621	
622	The remaining phase-3 checklist item "Variable-size outputs
623	work via the large-value heap" is now satisfied end-to-end at
624	the slot-store boundary. A graph fixture that exercises the
625	path on the dispatch side — a Lua box returning a multi-KB
626	string and a downstream box reading it — is a one-line fixture
627	addition and lands with the 6-more-integration-fixtures task.
628	
629	What's still deferred within 302:
630	- **Size-class free lists with coalescing.** Still no need.
631	- **Per-allocation free in the large-value heap.** The heap
632	  grows monotonically for the life of a run. Long-running
633	  iterator loops that emit large values will pin memory until
634	  run end. Acceptable for current fixtures; a size-class
635	  allocator with eager-coalesce is the upgrade path if profiling
636	  flags it.
637	
638	### Retraction of the monotonic-growth deferral — 2026-05-19
639	
640	The two deferrals listed immediately above this entry no longer
641	reflect the design. Monotonic growth was being described as
642	"acceptable for current fixtures." It isn't acceptable — it's a
643	memory leak by design, and any long-running workload (multi-day
644	runs, anything driven by an LLM in a loop, anything that
645	accumulates) will reproduce it as a real problem. Calling it
646	deferred made it easier to skip; calling it a leak makes it clear
647	why the skip isn't safe.
648	
649	The design above this implementation log has been rewritten
650	accordingly. There are no longer two stores. There is one
651	allocator that serves both compile-time-known sizes and
652	runtime-only-known sizes from a shared pool, with reference
653	counting driving deallocation, an O(1) eager neighbor-merge on
654	return, and a deeper sweep on the two triggers described in the
655	allocator section ("at the moment an allocation would otherwise
656	fail" and "at quiescence between task batches"). Both triggers
657	are deterministic — they react to the program's own behavior
658	rather than to wall-clock time.
659	
660	What this means in terms of work remaining:
661	
662	- The chained-block region currently serving variable-size values
663	  gets replaced by the unified allocator above. The slot-store
664	  cells that hold large-value pointers stay; what they point into
665	  changes from the bump-allocated chain to the size-class
666	  allocator's chunks.
667	- The slot store's small-fixed-size path adopts the same
668	  allocator. Today that path malloc's each slot's cell-array
669	  separately; under the new design every cell-array is a chunk
670	  from the unified allocator.
671	- The dispatch layer learns to increment and decrement reference
672	  counts when it hands values from producer to consumer. This is
673	  the widest change in scope — many places — but each individual
674	  change is one line. The new allocator depends on these calls
675	  being correct: a missed decrement leaks; a missed increment
676	  frees a value that's still in use.
677	- The address-ordered chunk list (described in the allocator
678	  section) is added alongside the size-class lists.
679	- The deeper sweep is implemented and wired into the two trigger
680	  points.
681	
682	The implementation has not been started; this entry is the
683	design commitment, not a completion notice. The earlier
684	"acceptable for current fixtures" framing is withdrawn.
685	
```

```
1	/* src/016-unified-allocator.h — the one allocator described in
2	 * issue 302's "Allocation strategy" section.
3	 *
4	 * What this module does, in plain terms:
5	 *
6	 *   Before the run starts, the loader writes down every distinct
7	 *   output-size declared by any box in the graph and how many boxes
8	 *   need each size. ua_create takes that list and pre-builds one
9	 *   free-list per distinct size, populated with enough chunks to
10	 *   cover the demand the loader counted.
11	 *
12	 *   During the run, ua_alloc(size) finds the smallest pre-built
13	 *   class whose chunks are large enough and hands one back. If the
14	 *   class is empty (demand exceeded what the loader pre-warmed) or
15	 *   the request is larger than any declared class (a runtime-only
16	 *   size), the allocator grows by getting another region from the
17	 *   operating system.
18	 *
19	 *   Every chunk handed out carries a reference count. ua_ref bumps
20	 *   it; ua_unref drops it; when the count hits zero, the chunk goes
21	 *   back to its free-list AND the deallocator checks the chunk's
22	 *   immediate neighbors in memory. If either neighbor is also free,
23	 *   they fuse into one larger chunk that goes onto the appropriate
24	 *   larger free-list. This is the "eager neighbor merge" from the
25	 *   issue file — O(1) and runs on every free.
26	 *
27	 *   When ua_alloc can't find a fitting chunk from any free-list,
28	 *   ua_sweep runs first (walks the address-ordered chunk list
29	 *   looking for runs of free chunks that the cheap neighbor-merge
30	 *   couldn't catch, fuses them). If the sweep produces a fitting
31	 *   chunk, the allocation succeeds without growing. If not, the
32	 *   heap grows. This is the "mandatory deep sweep at allocation
33	 *   failure" trigger from the issue file.
34	 *
35	 *   The quiescence trigger described in the issue file is not in
36	 *   this stage — the dispatch layer will call ua_sweep at its own
37	 *   quiescence points when that integration happens. For now the
38	 *   only automatic sweep is the one inside ua_alloc.
39	 *
40	 * What this module does NOT do (yet):
41	 *   - It does not integrate with the slot store or the dispatch
42	 *     layer. Those are later stages of the implementation. The
43	 *     existing chained-block region for variable-size payloads
44	 *     remains in use until those stages land.
45	 *   - It does not slice oversized chunks (a 2000-byte request that
46	 *     pops a 4096-byte chunk does not get the 2096-byte tail
47	 *     pushed back as a residual). The eager neighbor-merge and the
48	 *     pre-warmed class sizes cover the common case; slicing is a
49	 *     future optimization the API supports without source changes.
50	 *   - It uses one allocator-wide mutex instead of per-class locks.
51	 *     The issue file describes per-class locks as the optimization
52	 *     path; this stage keeps the locking simple and correct.
53	 *
54	 * Designed in issue 302's rewrite (2026-05-19).
55	 */
56	
57	#ifndef SORAMECH_UNIFIED_ALLOCATOR_H
58	#define SORAMECH_UNIFIED_ALLOCATOR_H
59	
60	#include <stddef.h>
61	#include <stdint.h>
62	
63	#ifdef __cplusplus
64	extern "C" {
65	#endif
66	
67	/* {{{ Opaque types */
68	typedef struct ua       ua_t;        /* the heap */
69	typedef struct ua_chunk ua_chunk_t;  /* one allocation */
70	/* }}} */
71	
72	/* {{{ Declared size classes */
73	/* A single (size, count) pair telling the allocator: "the graph
74	 * needs `count` chunks of `size` bytes." */
75	typedef struct ua_class_decl {
76	    size_t   size;
77	    uint32_t prewarm_count;
78	} ua_class_decl_t;
79	/* }}} */
80	
81	/* {{{ Lifecycle */
82	/* Construct a new heap.
83	 *
84	 *   classes        — array of declared size classes from graph analysis,
85	 *                    or NULL/zero if no pre-warming is wanted. Sizes
86	 *                    do not need to be sorted; the allocator sorts
87	 *                    them internally. Duplicate sizes are merged
88	 *                    (counts summed).
89	 *   n_classes      — number of entries in `classes`.
90	 *
91	 * Returns NULL on allocation failure or invalid arguments (e.g. any
92	 * class with size == 0). */
93	ua_t *ua_create(const ua_class_decl_t *classes, size_t n_classes);
94	
95	/* Free every region and the heap struct. Safe on NULL. After this
96	 * call, every chunk previously returned by ua_alloc on this heap is
97	 * invalid. */
98	void  ua_destroy(ua_t *h);
99	/* }}} */
100	
101	/* {{{ Allocation */
102	/* Allocate a chunk of at least `size` bytes. The returned chunk has
103	 * reference count 1; the caller is the initial owner.
104	 *
105	 * Lookup is "smallest free-list whose class size >= requested." If
106	 * no free-list has a chunk and the sweep can't produce one, the
107	 * heap grows by getting a new region from the OS.
108	 *
109	 * Returns NULL on allocation failure or if h is NULL or size is 0. */
110	ua_chunk_t *ua_alloc(ua_t *h, size_t size);
111	
112	/* Bump the chunk's reference count by 1. Safe on NULL chunk
113	 * (no-op). Returns the chunk for chaining. */
114	ua_chunk_t *ua_ref(ua_chunk_t *chunk);
115	
116	/* Drop the chunk's reference count by 1. If the count reaches zero
117	 * the chunk is returned to its free-list and an O(1) eager merge
118	 * with physical neighbors runs. Safe on NULL chunk (no-op). */
119	void ua_unref(ua_t *h, ua_chunk_t *chunk);
120	
121	/* Direct accessors. Both safe on NULL (data returns NULL, size
122	 * returns 0). */
123	void   *ua_data(ua_chunk_t *chunk);
124	size_t  ua_size(ua_chunk_t *chunk);
125	/* }}} */
126	
127	/* {{{ Sweep */
128	/* Walk the address-ordered chunk list and fuse runs of contiguous
129	 * free chunks that the eager neighbor-merge couldn't catch (e.g.
130	 * because the middle chunk in a 1-2-3 free run only got merged with
131	 * one neighbor at unref time, leaving the other free chunk on a
132	 * different list).
133	 *
134	 * The allocator runs this automatically before growing the heap.
135	 * Callers may also invoke it directly at quiescence points (e.g.
136	 * between dispatch batches). Returns the number of merges
137	 * performed. */
138	uint32_t ua_sweep(ua_t *h);
139	/* }}} */
140	
141	/* {{{ Stats (test / debug / future telemetry) */
142	uint64_t ua_bytes_in_use(const ua_t *h);
143	uint64_t ua_bytes_free  (const ua_t *h);
144	uint64_t ua_bytes_total (const ua_t *h);  /* sum of every region's capacity */
145	uint32_t ua_region_count(const ua_t *h);
146	uint32_t ua_alloc_calls (const ua_t *h);
147	uint32_t ua_free_calls  (const ua_t *h);  /* unrefs that reached zero */
148	uint32_t ua_neighbor_merges(const ua_t *h);
149	uint32_t ua_sweep_merges(const ua_t *h);
150	uint32_t ua_grow_calls  (const ua_t *h);
151	
152	/* For tests: how many free chunks currently sit on the free-list
153	 * for the class whose chunks are exactly `size` bytes. Returns 0
154	 * if no such class exists or the list is empty. */
155	uint32_t ua_class_free_count(const ua_t *h, size_t size);
156	/* }}} */
157	
158	#ifdef __cplusplus
159	}
160	#endif
161	
162	#endif /* SORAMECH_UNIFIED_ALLOCATOR_H */
163	
```

1	/* src/015-large-value-heap.h — variable-size payload allocator.
2	 *
3	 * The large-value heap is a chunked arena: a linked list of malloc'd
4	 * chunks where each allocation bump-allocates within the current
5	 * chunk. When a chunk fills up, a new chunk is allocated and prepended.
6	 * Allocations are never individually freed — the entire heap is
7	 * destroyed at run end (same lifetime model as the slot store).
8	 *
9	 * Why a separate heap, not just bigger slot cells:
10	 *
11	 *   The slot store gives every cell a fixed cell_capacity decided at
12	 *   slot_alloc time. For values whose size is not known at compile
13	 *   time — long LLM responses, dynamically-sized arrays, binary
14	 *   blobs of unbounded length — there is no useful upper bound to
15	 *   bake into cell_capacity.
16	 *
17	 *   The large-value heap solves this by decoupling cell size from
18	 *   payload size. A slot allocated with SLOT_FLAG_LARGE_VALUE
19	 *   (issue 302) stores only a handle in its cell; the payload bytes
20	 *   live in the large-value heap and the cell carries a stable
21	 *   pointer to them. Push allocates a heap region of the requested
22	 *   size; pop follows the pointer.
23	 *
24	 * Why stable pointers matter: the heap may grow by appending new
25	 * chunks, but existing chunks never move, so a pointer returned by
26	 * lvh_alloc remains valid for the life of the heap. This is what
27	 * makes the design safe for concurrent producers and consumers —
28	 * the consumer's pointer doesn't get invalidated by a later
29	 * producer's allocation.
30	 *
31	 * Concurrency: lvh_alloc is thread-safe via an internal mutex.
32	 * Reads of returned pointers need no synchronization beyond what
33	 * the slot store already provides at the cell level.
34	 *
35	 * Cleanup: allocations are not individually freed. The heap is
36	 * monotonic-growth for the duration of a run. For long-running
37	 * graphs with iterators producing large values in a tight loop,
38	 * this means the heap grows in proportion to total bytes produced
39	 * — not in proportion to peak live bytes. That's a known trade-off;
40	 * a proper free-on-pop allocator is a future iteration. Track
41	 * total_allocated() to surface when it matters.
42	 */
43	
44	#ifndef SORAMECH_LARGE_VALUE_HEAP_H
45	#define SORAMECH_LARGE_VALUE_HEAP_H
46	
47	#include <stddef.h>
48	#include <stdint.h>
49	
50	#ifdef __cplusplus
51	extern "C" {
52	#endif
53	
54	/* {{{ Type */
55	typedef struct lvh lvh_t;
56	/* }}} */
57	
58	/* {{{ Lifecycle */
59	/* Construct a new heap.
60	 *
61	 *   default_chunk_size — bytes per new chunk when the heap grows.
62	 *                        Pass 0 to use the built-in default (64 KB).
63	 *
64	 * Returns NULL on allocation failure. */
65	lvh_t  *lvh_create(size_t default_chunk_size);
66	
67	/* Free every chunk and the heap struct. Safe on NULL. After this
68	 * call, all pointers returned by lvh_alloc on this heap are
69	 * invalid. */
70	void    lvh_destroy(lvh_t *h);
71	/* }}} */
72	
73	/* {{{ Allocation */
74	/* Allocate `size` bytes of unspecified contents. The returned
75	 * pointer is stable for the life of the heap. Returns NULL on
76	 * allocation failure (out of memory at the malloc layer; the
77	 * heap itself doesn't pre-cap).
78	 *
79	 * Allocations larger than `default_chunk_size` get a dedicated
80	 * chunk sized exactly to the request — no waste, no fragmentation
81	 * of the regular chunks. Allocations smaller or equal share the
82	 * current bump chunk. */
83	void   *lvh_alloc(lvh_t *h, size_t size);
84	/* }}} */
85	
86	/* {{{ Stats (test / debug) */
87	/* Sum of every chunk's capacity (the working-set ceiling). */
88	uint64_t lvh_total_allocated(lvh_t *h);
89	
90	/* Sum of bytes returned to callers via lvh_alloc. */
91	uint64_t lvh_total_used(lvh_t *h);
92	
93	/* Count of chunks currently in the heap. */
94	uint32_t lvh_chunk_count(lvh_t *h);
95	/* }}} */
96	
97	#ifdef __cplusplus
98	}
99	#endif
100	
101	#endif /* SORAMECH_LARGE_VALUE_HEAP_H */
102	
```

1	/* src/009-slot-store.h — per-input-port slot store, public API.
2	 *
3	 * What it is, in a sentence: a thread-safe pool of ring-buffer
4	 * slots, allocated at graph load time, used by the phase 3
5	 * dispatch layer to route values between boxes.
6	 *
7	 * Slots belong to input ports, not tasks. Each box has one slot
8	 * per input port; the slot persists for the whole run. A wire is
9	 * a routing declaration — when a producer's task finishes, the
10	 * dispatch layer pushes copies of the output into every
11	 * downstream input slot. Fan-out is N pushes; fan-in is N
12	 * producers pushing into one slot. Designed in issue 302.
13	 *
14	 * Three slot modes, chosen via flags at slot_alloc:
15	 *
16	 *   1-cell peek (n_cells == 1, no flags)
17	 *     — for literals and wires from producers that run exactly
18	 *       once. Push once at startup. Every consumer task peeks
19	 *       without draining; the cell stays filled.
20	 *
21	 *   N-cell pop (n_cells > 1, no flags)
22	 *     — for wires whose producer runs many times (iterators,
23	 *       queued inputs). Push appends to tail; pop drains head
24	 *       FIFO. Consumer task pops one cell per spawn.
25	 *
26	 *   N-cell pop, tagged (SLOT_TAGGED)
27	 *     — same shape as N-cell pop, but pop returns the cell with
28	 *       the lowest tag, not the head. Used downstream of parallel
29	 *       iterators where push order is non-deterministic but
30	 *       consumption order must follow the iterator counter.
31	 *
32	 *   atomic counter (SLOT_ATOMIC_COUNTER, n_cells and cell_capacity
33	 *     ignored)
34	 *     — single atomic uint32. Read-and-increment via
35	 *       slot_read_inc(). Used for iterator / randomizer /
36	 *       weighted-routing counters. Concurrent reads each get a
37	 *       distinct value mod the caller-supplied bound.
38	 *
39	 *   large-value (SLOT_FLAG_LARGE_VALUE)
40	 *     — for outputs whose size is not known at compile time
41	 *       (multi-MB LLM responses, dynamically-sized arrays). The
42	 *       cell holds only a pointer; the payload bytes live in a
43	 *       large-value heap owned by the slot store. cell_capacity
44	 *       is ignored — push/pop accept any size. May be combined
45	 *       with SLOT_FLAG_TAGGED to get lowest-tag pop semantics
46	 *       over variable-size values.
47	 *
48	 * Synchronization is per-slot. There is no allocator-wide lock on
49	 * the hot read/write path. The atomic-counter slot uses
50	 * atomic_fetch_add; the ring-buffer slots use a per-slot spinlock
51	 * around header mutation (one byte, atomic_flag).
52	 *
53	 * Lifetime is the run. Slots are freed when the store is
54	 * destroyed at the end of the run — there is no per-slot refcount
55	 * and no end-of-stream propagation. Run termination is governed
56	 * by the pool's active-task counter (issue 301).
57	 *
58	 * Errors: a return of -1 from a read/write op means the operation
59	 * could not be performed (empty pop, buffer too small, type
60	 * mismatch). The store does not assert or abort; the dispatch
61	 * layer is expected to check return codes. The hard-crash policy
62	 * applies at the language-spec layer, not here.
63	 *
64	 * What's NOT in this version:
65	 *  - Size-class free lists and coalescing. The design in issue 302
66	 *    describes a segregated allocator with eager-coalesce; this
67	 *    initial implementation uses plain malloc per slot. The pattern
68	 *    is "allocate once at graph load, free at run end", so free-list
69	 *    optimization buys nothing for the current usage. Lands as a
70	 *    follow-on if profiling justifies it.
71	 *
72	 * Large-value heap support landed 2026-05-19: slots with
73	 * SLOT_FLAG_LARGE_VALUE store payload bytes in an internal
74	 * large-value heap (see src/015-large-value-heap.h) and the cell
75	 * only holds a stable pointer. The store lazy-creates the heap on
76	 * the first LARGE_VALUE slot_alloc and destroys it with the store.
77	 */
78	
79	#ifndef SORAMECH_SLOT_STORE_H
80	#define SORAMECH_SLOT_STORE_H
81	
82	#include <stdint.h>
83	
84	#ifdef __cplusplus
85	extern "C" {
86	#endif
87	
88	/* {{{ Types & flags */
89	typedef struct slot_store slot_store_t;
90	typedef int32_t           slot_id_t;
91	
92	#define SLOT_INVALID  ((slot_id_t)-1)
93	
94	/* slot_alloc flags */
95	enum {
96	    SLOT_FLAG_NONE           = 0,
97	    SLOT_FLAG_TAGGED         = 1 << 0,
98	    SLOT_FLAG_ATOMIC_COUNTER = 1 << 1,
99	    SLOT_FLAG_LARGE_VALUE    = 1 << 2,
100	};
101	/* }}} */
102	
103	/* {{{ Store lifecycle */
104	/* Construct an empty store. Returns NULL on allocation failure. */
105	slot_store_t *slot_store_create(void);
106	
107	/* Free every slot and the store itself. Safe to call on NULL. */
108	void          slot_store_destroy(slot_store_t *s);
109	/* }}} */
110	
111	/* {{{ Slot lifecycle */
112	/* Allocate a slot.
113	 *
114	 *   cell_capacity — bytes per cell. Ignored for SLOT_FLAG_ATOMIC_COUNTER.
115	 *   n_cells       — ring size (1 for peek slots, >1 for pop). Ignored for
116	 *                   SLOT_FLAG_ATOMIC_COUNTER.
117	 *   flags         — zero, SLOT_FLAG_TAGGED, or SLOT_FLAG_ATOMIC_COUNTER.
118	 *
119	 * Returns the new slot's id, or SLOT_INVALID on allocation failure or
120	 * invalid arguments. Ids are stable for the lifetime of the store. */
121	slot_id_t     slot_alloc(slot_store_t *s,
122	                         int32_t cell_capacity,
123	                         int32_t n_cells,
124	                         int32_t flags);
125	/* }}} */
126	
127	/* {{{ Ring-buffer ops */
128	/* Push a value into the slot's next free cell.
129	 *
130	 *   data, size — value bytes. size must be <= cell_capacity.
131	 *   tag        — ordering tag. Carried only on SLOT_FLAG_TAGGED slots;
132	 *                ignored otherwise.
133	 *
134	 * Returns 0 on success, -1 if the slot is full or arguments invalid.
135	 * Not valid on SLOT_FLAG_ATOMIC_COUNTER slots (returns -1). */
136	int           slot_push(slot_store_t *s, slot_id_t id,
137	                        const void *data, int32_t size, uint32_t tag);
138	
139	/* Peek at the head cell without draining it.
140	 *
141	 *   buf, buf_size — destination. The number of bytes copied is min of
142	 *                   the cell's filled_size and buf_size.
143	 *
144	 * Returns the cell's filled_size on success, -1 if the slot is empty,
145	 * the buffer is too small, or the slot is the wrong type. The cell
146	 * remains filled and may be re-peeked by subsequent tasks (1-cell peek
147	 * mode). */
148	int32_t       slot_peek(slot_store_t *s, slot_id_t id,
149	                        void *buf, int32_t buf_size);
150	
151	/* Drain one cell from the slot.
152	 *
153	 *   buf, buf_size — destination.
154	 *
155	 * For untagged slots, returns the head cell and advances head FIFO.
156	 * For SLOT_FLAG_TAGGED slots, returns the cell with the lowest tag
157	 * among the filled cells and marks that cell empty (head advances as
158	 * front-of-ring cells empty).
159	 *
160	 * Returns the cell's filled_size on success, -1 on empty / wrong type
161	 * / buffer too small. */
162	int32_t       slot_pop(slot_store_t *s, slot_id_t id,
163	                       void *buf, int32_t buf_size);
164	
165	/* Atomic read-and-increment on SLOT_FLAG_ATOMIC_COUNTER slots.
166	 *
167	 *   mod — modulus for the returned value. Pass UINT32_MAX for "no
168	 *         modulus".
169	 *
170	 * Returns the pre-increment counter value mod the supplied bound.
171	 * Concurrent readers each get a distinct value (the underlying
172	 * counter is incremented atomically). Returns UINT32_MAX on type
173	 * mismatch — the dispatch layer is expected to call this op only
174	 * on slots it allocated with SLOT_FLAG_ATOMIC_COUNTER. */
175	uint32_t      slot_read_inc(slot_store_t *s, slot_id_t id, uint32_t mod);
176	
177	/* Check whether the slot has any filled cell available for read.
178	 * Used by the dispatch layer's spawn-on-input-ready rule (issue 304).
179	 *
180	 * Returns 1 if a value is available, 0 otherwise. For ATOMIC_COUNTER
181	 * slots, always returns 1 (the counter is always readable). */
182	int           slot_has_value(slot_store_t *s, slot_id_t id);
183	/* }}} */
184	
185	/* {{{ Inspection (test / debug) */
186	/* Number of filled cells. For ATOMIC_COUNTER slots, returns 0. */
187	int32_t       slot_fill_count(slot_store_t *s, slot_id_t id);
188	
189	/* Slot count in the store. */
190	int32_t       slot_store_size(slot_store_t *s);
191	/* }}} */
192	
193	#ifdef __cplusplus
194	}
195	#endif
196	
197	#endif /* SORAMECH_SLOT_STORE_H */
198	
```

1	/* src/009-slot-store.c — per-input-port slot store, implementation.
2	 *
3	 * Three slot shapes coexist behind one set of APIs:
4	 *
5	 *   - Ring-buffer slots (peek and pop modes) — a header followed by
6	 *     n_cells cells laid out contiguously. Each cell is a 4-byte
7	 *     filled_size, optionally a 4-byte tag (for SLOT_FLAG_TAGGED),
8	 *     then cell_capacity bytes of payload. Untagged slots use the
9	 *     standard head/tail ring discipline; tagged slots use head/tail
10	 *     for the bookkeeping of "where to put the next push" but pop
11	 *     scans for the lowest-tag filled cell.
12	 *
13	 *   - Atomic-counter slots — no header bookkeeping, no payload cells;
14	 *     just one atomic_uint that slot_read_inc fetches-and-adds. No
15	 *     lock needed.
16	 *
17	 * Per-slot concurrency is a single atomic_flag spinlock for the
18	 * ring-buffer slots. Inside the lock the header and cells are mutated
19	 * without atomics; outside the lock no header bytes are touched.
20	 *
21	 * The store itself owns a flat array of slot pointers indexed by
22	 * slot_id_t. The array grows on demand. Ids are never reused — there
23	 * is no slot_free; slots live for the run.
24	 *
25	 * Designed in issue 302. This file implements the API declared in
26	 * 009-slot-store.h. Free-list optimization and the large-value heap
27	 * are deferred per the header's "what's not in this version" note.
28	 */
29	
30	#include "009-slot-store.h"
31	#include "015-large-value-heap.h"
32	
33	#include <stdatomic.h>
34	#include <stdint.h>
35	#include <stdlib.h>
36	#include <string.h>
37	
38	/* {{{ Internal slot record */
39	typedef struct slot_rec {
40	    uint32_t    cell_capacity;   /* bytes per cell payload (ring slots) */
41	    uint32_t    n_cells;         /* ring size (ring slots)              */
42	    _Atomic uint32_t head;       /* read index, mod n_cells             */
43	    _Atomic uint32_t tail;       /* write index, mod n_cells            */
44	    atomic_flag lock;            /* per-slot spinlock for header+cells  */
45	    uint32_t    flags;           /* SLOT_FLAG_*                         */
46	    uint32_t    cell_record;     /* derived: 4 (+4 if tagged) + cap     */
47	
48	    /* For atomic-counter slots: a single atomic uint, used by
49	     * slot_read_inc. Lives separately from `cells` so the two slot
50	     * shapes don't share storage interpretation. */
51	    _Atomic uint32_t counter;
52	
53	    /* For ring slots: n_cells * cell_record bytes. Flexible array. */
54	    uint8_t     cells[];
55	} slot_rec_t;
56	/* }}} */
57	
58	/* {{{ Internal store */
59	struct slot_store {
60	    slot_rec_t **slots;        /* dynamic array, indexed by slot_id_t */
61	    int32_t      count;
62	    int32_t      capacity;
63	
64	    /* Lazy-created on first SLOT_FLAG_LARGE_VALUE allocation. The
65	     * heap owns payload bytes for every LARGE_VALUE slot in this
66	     * store. Destroyed alongside the store. */
67	    lvh_t       *heap;
68	};
69	/* }}} */
70	
71	/* {{{ Local helpers — locking */
72	static inline void lock_slot(slot_rec_t *r)
73	{
74	    while (atomic_flag_test_and_set_explicit(&r->lock, memory_order_acquire)) {
75	        /* spin — contention is expected to be brief because the
76	         * locked section is just a header update plus a memcpy. */
77	    }
78	}
79	
80	static inline void unlock_slot(slot_rec_t *r)
81	{
82	    atomic_flag_clear_explicit(&r->lock, memory_order_release);
83	}
84	/* }}} */
85	
86	/* {{{ Local helpers — cell layout */
87	/* Pointer to the i-th cell within the slot's flexible array. The cell
88	 * record layout for ring slots is:
89	 *
90	 *   bytes 0..3   : filled_size (uint32_t; 0 means empty)
91	 *   bytes 4..7   : tag         (uint32_t; SLOT_FLAG_TAGGED *or*
92	 *                               SLOT_FLAG_LARGE_VALUE — the latter
93	 *                               keeps the 8-byte payload offset
94	 *                               consistent so the inline pointer is
95	 *                               always aligned)
96	 *   bytes (4|8)..: payload     (cell_capacity bytes; for LARGE_VALUE
97	 *                               this is a stable lvh-heap pointer)
98	 */
99	static inline uint8_t *cell_ptr(slot_rec_t *r, uint32_t i)
100	{
101	    return r->cells + (size_t)i * r->cell_record;
102	}
103	
104	static inline uint32_t cell_filled(slot_rec_t *r, uint32_t i)
105	{
106	    uint32_t v;
107	    memcpy(&v, cell_ptr(r, i), sizeof v);
108	    return v;
109	}
110	
111	static inline uint32_t cell_tag(slot_rec_t *r, uint32_t i)
112	{
113	    uint32_t v;
114	    memcpy(&v, cell_ptr(r, i) + 4, sizeof v);
115	    return v;
116	}
117	
118	/* The payload offset within a cell is 8 if there's a tag header OR
119	 * if the slot is LARGE_VALUE (we always reserve 4 bytes of pad for
120	 * LARGE_VALUE so the inline pointer is 8-byte aligned). Otherwise
121	 * it's 4. */
122	static inline uint8_t *cell_data(slot_rec_t *r, uint32_t i)
123	{
124	    int has_8b_header = (r->flags & SLOT_FLAG_TAGGED)
125	                     || (r->flags & SLOT_FLAG_LARGE_VALUE);
126	    return cell_ptr(r, i) + (has_8b_header ? 8 : 4);
127	}
128	/* }}} */
129	
130	/* {{{ slot_store_create() */
131	slot_store_t *slot_store_create(void)
132	{
133	    slot_store_t *s = calloc(1, sizeof *s);
134	    if (!s) return NULL;
135	    s->capacity = 16;
136	    s->slots    = calloc((size_t)s->capacity, sizeof(slot_rec_t *));
137	    if (!s->slots) { free(s); return NULL; }
138	    return s;
139	}
140	/* }}} */
141	
142	/* {{{ slot_store_destroy() */
143	void slot_store_destroy(slot_store_t *s)
144	{
145	    if (!s) return;
146	    for (int32_t i = 0; i < s->count; i++) {
147	        free(s->slots[i]);
148	    }
149	    free(s->slots);
150	    /* The lvh heap owns every LARGE_VALUE payload across every slot
151	     * in this store. Destroying it last is fine because slot
152	     * records no longer reference any heap pointers. */
153	    if (s->heap) lvh_destroy(s->heap);
154	    free(s);
155	}
156	/* }}} */
157	
158	/* {{{ slot_store_size() */
159	int32_t slot_store_size(slot_store_t *s)
160	{
161	    return s ? s->count : 0;
162	}
163	/* }}} */
164	
165	/* {{{ Local helper — grow_store_if_needed() */
166	static int grow_store_if_needed(slot_store_t *s)
167	{
168	    if (s->count < s->capacity) return 0;
169	    int32_t new_cap = s->capacity * 2;
170	    slot_rec_t **grown = realloc(s->slots, (size_t)new_cap * sizeof(slot_rec_t *));
171	    if (!grown) return -1;
172	    memset(grown + s->capacity, 0,
173	           (size_t)(new_cap - s->capacity) * sizeof(slot_rec_t *));
174	    s->slots    = grown;
175	    s->capacity = new_cap;
176	    return 0;
177	}
178	/* }}} */
179	
180	/* {{{ slot_alloc() */
181	slot_id_t slot_alloc(slot_store_t *s,
182	                     int32_t cell_capacity,
183	                     int32_t n_cells,
184	                     int32_t flags)
185	{
186	    if (!s) return SLOT_INVALID;
187	
188	    size_t bytes_cells = 0;
189	    uint32_t cell_record = 0;
190	    uint32_t stored_capacity = 0;
191	
192	    if (flags & SLOT_FLAG_ATOMIC_COUNTER) {
193	        /* Atomic-counter slot: no ring cells. cell_capacity and
194	         * n_cells are ignored. */
195	        bytes_cells = 0;
196	    } else if (flags & SLOT_FLAG_LARGE_VALUE) {
197	        /* Large-value slot: the cell holds a stable pointer into
198	         * the lvh heap, not the bytes themselves. Caller-supplied
199	         * cell_capacity is ignored (it has no meaning here — the
200	         * actual payload bytes live in the heap and can be any
201	         * size). n_cells controls ring depth as usual.
202	         *
203	         * Cell layout for LARGE_VALUE is 4 (filled_size) + 4
204	         * (tag or pad) + 8 (pointer) = 16 bytes; cell_data() uses
205	         * the 8-byte payload offset so the pointer is naturally
206	         * aligned. */
207	        if (n_cells <= 0) return SLOT_INVALID;
208	        stored_capacity = (uint32_t)sizeof(void *);
209	        cell_record     = 4u + 4u + (uint32_t)sizeof(void *);
210	        bytes_cells     = (size_t)n_cells * cell_record;
211	
212	        /* Lazy-create the heap on first LARGE_VALUE slot. Default
213	         * chunk size (64 KB) is fine for now — variable-size
214	         * payloads bigger than that get their own chunks per
215	         * lvh_alloc's oversized-allocation rule. */
216	        if (!s->heap) {
217	            s->heap = lvh_create(0);
218	            if (!s->heap) return SLOT_INVALID;
219	        }
220	    } else {
221	        if (cell_capacity <= 0 || n_cells <= 0) return SLOT_INVALID;
222	        stored_capacity = (uint32_t)cell_capacity;
223	        cell_record = (uint32_t)(4
224	                                  + ((flags & SLOT_FLAG_TAGGED) ? 4 : 0)
225	                                  + cell_capacity);
226	        bytes_cells = (size_t)n_cells * cell_record;
227	    }
228	
229	    slot_rec_t *r = calloc(1, sizeof(slot_rec_t) + bytes_cells);
230	    if (!r) return SLOT_INVALID;
231	
232	    r->cell_capacity = (flags & SLOT_FLAG_ATOMIC_COUNTER) ? 0 : stored_capacity;
233	    r->n_cells       = (flags & SLOT_FLAG_ATOMIC_COUNTER) ? 0 : (uint32_t)n_cells;
234	    r->cell_record   = cell_record;
235	    r->flags         = (uint32_t)flags;
236	    atomic_init(&r->head,    0u);
237	    atomic_init(&r->tail,    0u);
238	    atomic_init(&r->counter, 0u);
239	    /* atomic_flag has no atomic_flag_init in C11; ATOMIC_FLAG_INIT is
240	     * for initializers. Clearing it leaves it in the "not set" state. */
241	    atomic_flag_clear(&r->lock);
242	
243	    if (grow_store_if_needed(s) != 0) { free(r); return SLOT_INVALID; }
244	    slot_id_t id = s->count++;
245	    s->slots[id] = r;
246	    return id;
247	}
248	/* }}} */
249	
250	/* {{{ Local helper — get_slot() */
251	static slot_rec_t *get_slot(slot_store_t *s, slot_id_t id)
252	{
253	    if (!s || id < 0 || id >= s->count) return NULL;
254	    return s->slots[id];
255	}
256	/* }}} */
257	
258	/* {{{ slot_push() */
259	int slot_push(slot_store_t *s, slot_id_t id,
260	              const void *data, int32_t size, uint32_t tag)
261	{
262	    slot_rec_t *r = get_slot(s, id);
263	    if (!r)                                      return -1;
264	    if (r->flags & SLOT_FLAG_ATOMIC_COUNTER)     return -1;
265	    if (size < 0)                                return -1;
266	    if (!data && size > 0)                       return -1;
267	
268	    int large = (r->flags & SLOT_FLAG_LARGE_VALUE) != 0;
269	    /* For LARGE_VALUE slots size has no per-cell upper bound; the
270	     * heap absorbs whatever the caller wants to store. For
271	     * fixed-size slots, enforce the cell capacity as before. */
272	    if (!large && (uint32_t)size > r->cell_capacity) return -1;
273	
274	    /* For LARGE_VALUE, allocate the heap region BEFORE taking the
275	     * slot's spinlock. The heap has its own mutex; nesting the two
276	     * is fine but reducing time-under-spinlock keeps the lock
277	     * holder fast for everyone else. */
278	    void *heap_ptr = NULL;
279	    if (large && size > 0) {
280	        heap_ptr = lvh_alloc(s->heap, (size_t)size);
281	        if (!heap_ptr) return -1;
282	        memcpy(heap_ptr, data, (size_t)size);
283	    }
284	
285	    lock_slot(r);
286	
287	    uint32_t head = atomic_load_explicit(&r->head, memory_order_relaxed);
288	    uint32_t tail = atomic_load_explicit(&r->tail, memory_order_relaxed);
289	    if (tail - head >= r->n_cells) {
290	        /* Ring full. The heap allocation we just made stays alive
291	         * — there's no per-allocation free — but it's bytes the
292	         * caller will retry to use, so the cost is bounded by
293	         * how often pushes actually fail. */
294	        unlock_slot(r);
295	        return -1;
296	    }
297	
298	    uint32_t i = tail % r->n_cells;
299	    uint8_t *cp = cell_ptr(r, i);
300	
301	    /* filled_size */
302	    uint32_t fs = (uint32_t)size;
303	    memcpy(cp, &fs, sizeof fs);
304	    /* tag (only if tagged); LARGE_VALUE without TAGGED still has 4
305	     * pad bytes in the same position so the payload pointer stays
306	     * 8-byte aligned, but we leave them zero. */
307	    if (r->flags & SLOT_FLAG_TAGGED) {
308	        memcpy(cp + 4, &tag, sizeof tag);
309	    }
310	    /* payload */
311	    if (large) {
312	        /* Store the heap pointer (may be NULL for zero-size). */
313	        memcpy(cell_data(r, i), &heap_ptr, sizeof heap_ptr);
314	    } else if (size > 0) {
315	        memcpy(cell_data(r, i), data, (size_t)size);
316	    }
317	
318	    atomic_store_explicit(&r->tail, tail + 1u, memory_order_release);
319	    unlock_slot(r);
320	    return 0;
321	}
322	/* }}} */
323	
324	/* {{{ slot_peek() */
325	int32_t slot_peek(slot_store_t *s, slot_id_t id,
326	                  void *buf, int32_t buf_size)
327	{
328	    slot_rec_t *r = get_slot(s, id);
329	    if (!r)                                  return -1;
330	    if (r->flags & SLOT_FLAG_ATOMIC_COUNTER) return -1;
331	    if (buf_size < 0 || (!buf && buf_size > 0)) return -1;
332	
333	    int large = (r->flags & SLOT_FLAG_LARGE_VALUE) != 0;
334	
335	    lock_slot(r);
336	
337	    uint32_t head = atomic_load_explicit(&r->head, memory_order_acquire);
338	    uint32_t tail = atomic_load_explicit(&r->tail, memory_order_acquire);
339	    if (head == tail) {
340	        unlock_slot(r);
341	        return -1;
342	    }
343	
344	    uint32_t i = head % r->n_cells;
345	    uint32_t fs = cell_filled(r, i);
346	    if (fs == 0) {
347	        unlock_slot(r);
348	        return -1;
349	    }
350	    if ((uint32_t)buf_size < fs) {
351	        unlock_slot(r);
352	        return -1;
353	    }
354	    if (fs > 0) {
355	        if (large) {
356	            void *p;
357	            memcpy(&p, cell_data(r, i), sizeof p);
358	            if (p) memcpy(buf, p, fs);
359	        } else {
360	            memcpy(buf, cell_data(r, i), fs);
361	        }
362	    }
363	    /* head not advanced — peek leaves the cell in place. */
364	    unlock_slot(r);
365	    return (int32_t)fs;
366	}
367	/* }}} */
368	
369	/* {{{ Local helper — find_lowest_tag_index() */
370	/* Return the in-ring cell index with the lowest tag among filled
371	 * cells. The caller holds the slot lock. Returns UINT32_MAX if no
372	 * filled cells exist. */
373	static uint32_t find_lowest_tag_index(slot_rec_t *r)
374	{
375	    uint32_t best     = UINT32_MAX;
376	    uint32_t best_tag = UINT32_MAX;
377	    for (uint32_t i = 0; i < r->n_cells; i++) {
378	        if (cell_filled(r, i) == 0) continue;
379	        uint32_t t = cell_tag(r, i);
380	        if (best == UINT32_MAX || t < best_tag) {
381	            best     = i;
382	            best_tag = t;
383	        }
384	    }
385	    return best;
386	}
387	/* }}} */
388	
389	/* {{{ slot_pop() */
390	int32_t slot_pop(slot_store_t *s, slot_id_t id,
391	                 void *buf, int32_t buf_size)
392	{
393	    slot_rec_t *r = get_slot(s, id);
394	    if (!r)                                  return -1;
395	    if (r->flags & SLOT_FLAG_ATOMIC_COUNTER) return -1;
396	    if (buf_size < 0 || (!buf && buf_size > 0)) return -1;
397	
398	    lock_slot(r);
399	
400	    uint32_t pick;
401	    if (r->flags & SLOT_FLAG_TAGGED) {
402	        pick = find_lowest_tag_index(r);
403	        if (pick == UINT32_MAX) { unlock_slot(r); return -1; }
404	    } else {
405	        uint32_t head = atomic_load_explicit(&r->head, memory_order_acquire);
406	        uint32_t tail = atomic_load_explicit(&r->tail, memory_order_acquire);
407	        if (head == tail) { unlock_slot(r); return -1; }
408	        pick = head % r->n_cells;
409	    }
410	
411	    uint32_t fs = cell_filled(r, pick);
412	    if (fs == 0)                  { unlock_slot(r); return -1; }
413	    if ((uint32_t)buf_size < fs)  { unlock_slot(r); return -1; }
414	
415	    if (fs > 0) {
416	        if (r->flags & SLOT_FLAG_LARGE_VALUE) {
417	            void *p;
418	            memcpy(&p, cell_data(r, pick), sizeof p);
419	            if (p) memcpy(buf, p, fs);
420	        } else {
421	            memcpy(buf, cell_data(r, pick), fs);
422	        }
423	    }
424	
425	    /* Mark cell empty. The heap allocation (if any) stays alive in
426	     * the lvh heap — there's no per-allocation free in the current
427	     * design. */
428	    uint32_t zero = 0;
429	    memcpy(cell_ptr(r, pick), &zero, sizeof zero);
430	
431	    /* For untagged: advance head by one. For tagged: walk head
432	     * forward over any contiguous empty cells (the head/tail
433	     * relationship is preserved so further pushes still land at the
434	     * right spot, but stale empties at the front of the ring don't
435	     * block future pushes that wrap). */
436	    if (!(r->flags & SLOT_FLAG_TAGGED)) {
437	        uint32_t head = atomic_load_explicit(&r->head, memory_order_relaxed);
438	        atomic_store_explicit(&r->head, head + 1u, memory_order_release);
439	    } else {
440	        uint32_t head = atomic_load_explicit(&r->head, memory_order_relaxed);
441	        uint32_t tail = atomic_load_explicit(&r->tail, memory_order_relaxed);
442	        while (head < tail && cell_filled(r, head % r->n_cells) == 0) {
443	            head++;
444	        }
445	        atomic_store_explicit(&r->head, head, memory_order_release);
446	    }
447	
448	    unlock_slot(r);
449	    return (int32_t)fs;
450	}
451	/* }}} */
452	
453	/* {{{ slot_read_inc() */
454	uint32_t slot_read_inc(slot_store_t *s, slot_id_t id, uint32_t mod)
455	{
456	    slot_rec_t *r = get_slot(s, id);
457	    if (!r)                                     return UINT32_MAX;
458	    if (!(r->flags & SLOT_FLAG_ATOMIC_COUNTER)) return UINT32_MAX;
459	    if (mod == 0)                               return UINT32_MAX;
460	
461	    uint32_t prev = atomic_fetch_add_explicit(&r->counter, 1u,
462	                                              memory_order_relaxed);
463	    return prev % mod;
464	}
465	/* }}} */
466	
467	/* {{{ slot_has_value() */
468	int slot_has_value(slot_store_t *s, slot_id_t id)
469	{
470	    slot_rec_t *r = get_slot(s, id);
471	    if (!r) return 0;
472	    if (r->flags & SLOT_FLAG_ATOMIC_COUNTER) return 1;
473	
474	    if (r->flags & SLOT_FLAG_TAGGED) {
475	        /* Tagged: any filled cell counts. Cheap scan; tagged slots
476	         * are usually small. */
477	        lock_slot(r);
478	        int found = 0;
479	        for (uint32_t i = 0; i < r->n_cells; i++) {
480	            if (cell_filled(r, i) != 0) { found = 1; break; }
481	        }
482	        unlock_slot(r);
483	        return found;
484	    }
485	
486	    uint32_t head = atomic_load_explicit(&r->head, memory_order_acquire);
487	    uint32_t tail = atomic_load_explicit(&r->tail, memory_order_acquire);
488	    return head != tail;
489	}
490	/* }}} */
491	
492	/* {{{ slot_fill_count() */
493	int32_t slot_fill_count(slot_store_t *s, slot_id_t id)
494	{
495	    slot_rec_t *r = get_slot(s, id);
496	    if (!r)                                  return 0;
497	    if (r->flags & SLOT_FLAG_ATOMIC_COUNTER) return 0;
498	
499	    if (r->flags & SLOT_FLAG_TAGGED) {
500	        lock_slot(r);
501	        int32_t n = 0;
502	        for (uint32_t i = 0; i < r->n_cells; i++) {
503	            if (cell_filled(r, i) != 0) n++;
504	        }
505	        unlock_slot(r);
506	        return n;
507	    }
508	
509	    uint32_t head = atomic_load_explicit(&r->head, memory_order_acquire);
510	    uint32_t tail = atomic_load_explicit(&r->tail, memory_order_acquire);
511	    return (int32_t)(tail - head);
512	}
513	/* }}} */
514	
```
